# Orders (Sub-project 1) Implementation Plan

Steps use checkbox (`- [ ]`) syntax for tracking.

**Goal:** Customers browse with a price filter and per-store stock, check out (T1) for delivery or Click & Collect, follow their orders with a status timeline, and manage delivery addresses — on the real Clerk sign-in, with the order rules enforced by the database.

**Architecture:** Yuhang's branch (`4a69838`) is ported and adapted, not rewritten. The database gains the order rules (BR06–BR09, BR14, BR17 widened; new BR20), statement-level and deferred triggers, and three views; `app/services/orders.py` runs T1 as one `READ COMMITTED` transaction (lookup → claim → lines → price check → stock → charge → paid) and the order reads; the storefront pages are ported onto main's `useMe` / `ShopProvider` and Clerk-authenticated API client.

**Tech Stack:** PostgreSQL 18 (PL/pgSQL triggers, constraint triggers, views), FastAPI + psycopg2 (hand-written SQL), Pydantic v2, pytest; Next.js 16, React 19, openapi-fetch, `@clerk/nextjs`, Vitest.

**Spec:** `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-02-orders-design.md`

## Global Constraints

- Work only in the worktree `/Users/ethan/Dev/Course/comp9120/JBhifi/.claude/worktrees/ethan+orders` (branch `ethan/orders`). Run commands from its root unless a step says `web/`.
- Python: every Python command starts with `PY=/Users/ethan/Dev/Course/comp9120/JBhifi/.venv/bin/python`; tests: `$PY -m pytest -q -p no:cacheprovider` (rebuilds the `jbhifi_test` database from `sql/` each run).
- Database: reach the container with `docker exec -i jbhifi-db …`. Never run `docker compose` from the worktree, and never `docker compose down -v` anywhere: the running container belongs to the main checkout's compose project, which mounts the main checkout's `./sql` (branch `main`, not this branch), so a re-initialised volume would load the wrong SQL and lose the dev data. The dev database is rebuilt in place in Task 11, after asking the user.
- Hand-written SQL with `%s` parameters only; `psycopg2.sql` only for fixed fragments; never f-strings or `%` formatting in SQL.
- Invariants live in the database; one service call = one transaction (`get_cursor` commits at the end of the request, rolls back on any exception); routers stay thin; route handlers using psycopg2 are `def`; keep `DbCursor = Annotated[..., Depends(get_cursor, scope="function")]`.
- Every constraint is named; business rules are `brNN_<what>`; triggers `RAISE … USING ERRCODE = 'check_violation', CONSTRAINT = 'brNN_…'`; every BR has a violating test in `tests/test_business_rules.py` that asserts the name.
- Names are binding (spec §2 and §5): `br20_paid_in_full`, `br20_charge_while_placed`, `br20_one_charge_per_order`, `br08_order_line_immutable`, `br08_unit_price_immutable`, `br09_order_header_immutable`, `br14_payment_append_only`, `br09_history_append_only`, `br18_adjustment_append_only`, `br07_product_on_sale`, `br07_order_has_lines`, `br17_order_address_active`, `br06_collect_at_retail_store`, `br06_store_stays_retail`, `br09_history_first_row_is_creation`, `br09_history_status_changes`, `br09_history_written_by_trigger`; views `v_order_summary`, `v_order_payment`, `v_order_timeline`; error codes `price_changed`, `out_of_stock`, `customer_only`, `not_your_order`, `not_your_account`, `other_store`, `invalid_price_range`.
- Pydantic checks only shape: quantity ≤ 0, duplicate products and unknown stores are answered by the database (409 with the constraint name); an empty basket is a 422.
- Money is `NUMERIC(10,2)` / `Decimal`, sent as 2-decimal strings (`"0.00"`, never `"0"`). The web app computes in integer cents.
- T1 order of steps (spec §3): lookup → claim (`ON CONFLICT ON CONSTRAINT br10_order_idempotency_unique DO NOTHING`) → lines (`INSERT … SELECT` from `v_product_current_price`, `product_id` order) → price check against `expected_total` → stock (conditional relative `UPDATE`, `product_id` order, no product lock) → charge (`INSERT … SELECT` from `v_order_total` when > 0) → `paid` (after `set_config('app.staff_id', '', true)`).
- Beyond-Week-10 features are exactly the optional ones in spec §2 (`br20_one_charge_per_order`, `br07_order_has_lines`, `br09_history_written_by_trigger`, statement-level `TRUNCATE` triggers); nothing else beyond Week 10.
- Do not port from Yuhang's branch: request fingerprints / `app/services/idempotency.py`, deferred BR14 triggers / `flush_deferred` / `SET CONSTRAINTS ALL`, `SECURITY DEFINER`, `sql/05_permissions.sql`, checkout's product `FOR UPDATE`, the customer `FOR UPDATE` before the claim, the `OperationalError → 503` mapping, the recursive category tree, the demo session (`session.ts`, `ensureSession`, admin `session.tsx`), cancel and return UI (SP2/SP3).
- Next.js 16: read `web/AGENTS.md` and the relevant guide in `web/node_modules/next/dist/docs/` before writing Next code. After an API change used by the web app, start the API from the worktree and run `npm --prefix web run gen:api`; commit `web/src/lib/api/schema.d.ts`.
- Secrets: never print or commit `.env` / `web/.env.local`.
- Commits: plain sentences prefixed `Orders:`.
- Test counts in `Expected:` lines come from the planning run, which built Tasks 1–7 on a scratch copy. A count for
  one file or one `-k` selection is exact; a whole-suite total shifts by however many tests earlier tasks really
  added, so for the whole suite the contract is "all pass, no errors".

## Deviations from the spec

- Spec §8 step 1 puts the SQL rules, views and existing-test fixes in one commit. Tasks 1–3 make three commits, each
  with the whole suite green, so each group of rules can be reviewed (and rejected) on its own.
- Spec §8 step 5 rebuilds the dev database with `docker compose down -v && docker compose up -d`. Task 11 rebuilds
  it in place instead (`DROP DATABASE … WITH (FORCE)`, then `sql/*.sql` through `docker exec`), because compose
  would mount the main checkout's `./sql` (branch `main`), not this branch's. It is still asked before running.
- Spec §5 lists 401 and 403 for `GET /customers/{id}/orders`. Task 6 adds 404 `not_found` when support asks for a
  customer that doesn't exist (customers and store staff are refused before that).

## Review Focus

1. A guest fills the cart, opens `/checkout` and signs in there: the page loads for them and the cart still holds
   everything. Manual: Task 10 Step 14 row 1, run in Task 11 Step 5.
2. A send gets no answer (network, ≥ 500, 401, 403): until that try is answered, nothing is sent under a new key.
   "Try again" reuses its key and body; a changed review must send the earlier try again, or start a new order after
   a look at My orders; a refused basket (404/409/422) frees its key; blocked storage still keeps the key in
   memory. Task 8 `keeps the key unless the API refused the basket` and `keeps the attempt in this page's memory
   when storage is blocked`; Task 10 Step 14 rows 6 and 10–12.
3. Delivery shipped from a retail store that has the stock (the page pre-selects the warehouse, but any store may
   ship): it is placed, the stock comes from that store, and that store's staff can read it. Task 7
   `test_delivery_can_ship_from_a_retail_store_with_stock`.
4. Two Click & Collect checkouts at the same store at the same moment: neither waits for the other (BR06 reads the
   store `FOR SHARE`). Task 2 `test_collect_orders_at_one_store_do_not_wait_for_each_other`.
5. A key retried after its order has moved on (the store is picking it): 200 with that order as it is now, nothing
   placed, taken or charged again. Task 7 `test_a_key_retried_after_its_order_moved_on_still_replays_it`.

---

### Task 1: Purchase-record rules

BR20 is new: an order may move from placed to paid only when it has lines and has been charged exactly its total. The charge must be taken while the order is placed, and only once. BR08 widens: lines are added only while the order is placed, and a line is never changed or deleted. BR09 makes the order header fixed. Payments and the status history become append-only. The project's first statement-level triggers close `TRUNCATE` on four tables. Four existing tests pay "the wrong way round", and BR20 breaks them. They move onto a `pay()` helper **in the same commit as the SQL**.

**Files:**
- Modify: `sql/01_tables.sql` (one index after `CREATE TABLE payment (…);`, at the end of the file)
- Modify: `sql/02_rules.sql` (replace the `BR08` section; add to the `BR09` section; append a `BR20` section and an `append-only records` section at the end of the file)
- Modify / Test: `tests/test_business_rules.py`

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes (main):
  - tables `customer_order`, `order_line`, `payment`, `order_status_history`, `stock_adjustment`, `return_line`
  - view `v_order_total(order_id, line_count, total)` and domain `order_status`
  - in `tests/test_business_rules.py`:
    - `make_customer(cur, email, with_address=True) -> int`
    - `place_order(cur, customer_id, lines, method="click_collect", store_id=1, address_no=None) -> int`, where lines = `[(product_id, quantity, unit_price), …]`
    - `advance(cur, order_id, *statuses, staff_id=None)`
    - `request_return(cur, order_id, lines, days_from_now=0) -> int`
    - the constants `RETURN_REQUEST` and `ADJUSTMENT`
    - the fixtures `customer` and `collected_order`
  - conftest fixtures `cur`, `violates(constraint, query, params=None)` and `product_id(sku) -> int`.
- Produces:
  - **BR20 index.** Partial unique index `br20_one_charge_per_order ON payment (order_id) WHERE kind = 'charge'` in `sql/01_tables.sql`. A violation is SQLSTATE 23505 with `constraint_name` = the index name, so the API answers 409 with that code.
  - **BR20 paid in full.** `br20_check_paid_in_full()` + trigger `br20_paid_in_full`, `BEFORE UPDATE OF status ON customer_order`. Placed → paid needs ≥ 1 line and `COALESCE(sum of charges, 0) = v_order_total.total`.
  - **BR20 charge guard.** `br20_check_charge()` + trigger `br20_charge_while_placed`, `BEFORE INSERT ON payment`.
    - A charge is accepted only while the order is placed; the trigger locks the order `FOR UPDATE`.
    - Refunds are untouched.
    - A missing order is left to `payment_order_fk`.
  - **BR08 widened.** `br08_guard_order_line()` + trigger `br08_order_line_immutable`, `BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE OR DELETE ON order_line`. It replaces main's `br08_block_unit_price_change()` and its trigger `br08_unit_price_immutable`. It raises:
    - `br08_unit_price_immutable` for a price change (reported first);
    - `br08_order_line_immutable` for an insert after placed, any other change, and any delete.
    - A missing order is left to `order_line_order_fk`.
  - **BR09 header.** `br09_block_order_header_change()` + trigger `br09_order_header_immutable`, `BEFORE UPDATE ON customer_order`.
  - **Append-only records.** One shared function `block_append_only_change()`, which reports the constraint name passed as `TG_ARGV[0]`.
    - Row triggers `br14_payment_append_only` (payment) and `br09_history_append_only` (order_status_history).
    - Statement-level `BEFORE TRUNCATE` triggers `br14_payment_append_only_truncate`, `br09_history_append_only_truncate`, `br18_adjustment_append_only_truncate` and `br08_order_line_immutable_truncate`. They raise `br14_payment_append_only`, `br09_history_append_only`, `br18_adjustment_append_only` and `br08_order_line_immutable`.
  - **Test helper** `pay(cur, order_id)` in `tests/test_business_rules.py`: inserts the charge from `v_order_total` (only when total > 0), then calls `advance(cur, order_id, "paid")`.
  - **Rules later tasks must follow:**
    - To move an order to paid, first insert its single charge **while it is placed**, then update the status. The charge equals `v_order_total.total`; there is no charge when the total is 0. Task 6's `paid_order()` and Task 7's `checkout` follow this order.
    - Triggers with the same timing fire in name order:
      - `customer_order` BEFORE UPDATE: `br06_collect_at_retail_store` (only for `UPDATE OF fulfilment_method, fulfilling_store_id`), `br09_order_header_immutable`, `br09_order_status_transition`, `br20_paid_in_full`.
      - `payment` BEFORE INSERT: `br14_refunds_within_charged`, then `br20_charge_while_placed`.
      - `payment` BEFORE UPDATE: `br14_payment_append_only`, then `br14_refunds_within_charged`.
    - No order can be deleted any more. Every order has a history row, and the `ON DELETE CASCADE` into `order_line` / `order_status_history` is refused. Tests that commit orders can never clean them up.

- [ ] **Step 1: Move the existing order tests onto a `pay()` helper**

Why: with BR20 (Step 7), placed → paid is refused until the charge is in, and a charge is refused once the order has left placed. Main's tests charge after the move, or not at all. Run against the new SQL unchanged, they give **2 failed + 8 errors**:
- The `collected_order` fixture moves to paid before charging ("Order N can't be paid: 2 line(s), 527.95 due, 0.00 charged"). That errors 8 tests at setup.
- `test_br09_status_moves_only_along_allowed_transitions` and `test_br11_only_completed_orders_can_be_returned` move to paid with no charge, so they fail.

`test_br14_refunds` changes too. Its "charge that names a return" case would be answered by `br20_charge_while_placed`, because a BEFORE trigger runs before the CHECK. So that case moves to an order that is still placed.

In `tests/test_business_rules.py`, add this helper after `advance()` (before `RETURN_REQUEST = """`):

```python
def pay(cur, order_id):
    """Charge the order its total, then move it to paid, as checkout does (BR20). Nothing due: no charge row."""
    cur.execute(
        """
        INSERT INTO payment (order_id, kind, amount)
        SELECT order_id, 'charge', total FROM v_order_total WHERE order_id = %s AND total > 0
        """,
        (order_id,),
    )
    advance(cur, order_id, "paid")
```

```python
def test_br09_status_moves_only_along_allowed_transitions(cur, customer, product_id, violates):
    order_id = place_order(cur, customer, [(product_id("APL-AP4"), 1, "189.00")])
    update = "UPDATE customer_order SET status = %s WHERE order_id = %s"
    violates("br09_order_status_transition", update, ("collected", order_id))   # skips paid → picking → ready
    pay(cur, order_id)
    advance(cur, order_id, "picking")
    violates("br09_order_status_transition", update, ("shipped", order_id))     # delivery-only status
```

Replace `test_br14_refunds` with:

```python
def test_br14_refunds(cur, customer, collected_order, product_id, violates):
    return_id = request_return(cur, collected_order, [(1, 1)])
    cur.execute("""UPDATE return_request SET status = 'received', received_store_id = 1, processed_by_staff_id = 14
                   WHERE return_id = %s""", (return_id,))
    payment = "INSERT INTO payment (order_id, kind, amount, return_id) VALUES (%s, %s, %s, %s)"
    violates("br14_refund_has_return", payment, (collected_order, "refund", "189.00", None))
    # A charge that names a return. BR20 refuses any charge to the collected order first, so this is shown on
    # an order that is still placed; CHECKs run before foreign keys, so another order's return_id is fine here.
    placed = place_order(cur, customer, [(product_id("SONY-WH1000XM5-B"), 1, "399.00")])
    violates("br14_refund_has_return", payment, (placed, "charge", "1.00", return_id))
    violates("br14_refunds_within_charged", payment, (collected_order, "refund", "600.00", return_id))
    cur.execute(payment, (collected_order, "refund", "189.00", return_id))
    violates("br14_refund_once_per_return", payment, (collected_order, "refund", "10.00", return_id))
```

Run: `$PY -m pytest -q -p no:cacheprovider tests/test_business_rules.py`
Expected: `33 passed`. Main's SQL accepts either order of charge and move, so nothing changes yet.

After `test_br08_unit_price_never_changes`, add:

```python
def test_br08_lines_are_added_only_while_the_order_is_placed(cur, customer, product_id, violates):
    airpods, flip, headphones = product_id("APL-AP4"), product_id("JBL-FLIP7-B"), product_id("SONY-WH1000XM5-B")
    order_id = place_order(cur, customer, [(airpods, 2, "189.00")])
    line = "INSERT INTO order_line (order_id, line_no, product_id, quantity, unit_price) VALUES (%s, %s, %s, 1, %s)"
    # A written line is never changed or deleted, not even while its order is still placed...
    violates("br08_order_line_immutable",
             "UPDATE order_line SET quantity = 1 WHERE order_id = %s AND line_no = 1", (order_id,))
    violates("br08_order_line_immutable",
             "UPDATE order_line SET product_id = %s WHERE order_id = %s AND line_no = 1", (flip, order_id))
    violates("br08_unit_price_immutable",          # a price change is named as such, whatever else changes
             "UPDATE order_line SET unit_price = 1.00, quantity = 1 WHERE order_id = %s AND line_no = 1", (order_id,))
    violates("br08_order_line_immutable", "DELETE FROM order_line WHERE order_id = %s", (order_id,))
    # Row triggers don't fire on TRUNCATE. (Without CASCADE it is refused anyway: return_line refers to order_line.)
    violates("br08_order_line_immutable", "TRUNCATE order_line CASCADE")
    # ...and lines are added while the order is placed, and only then.
    cur.execute(line, (order_id, 2, flip, "149.95"))
    pay(cur, order_id)
    violates("br08_order_line_immutable", line, (order_id, 3, headphones, "399.00"))
    violates("order_line_order_fk", line, (-1, 1, flip, "149.95"))       # no such order: the foreign key says so
```

After `test_br09_every_status_change_is_logged_with_who_made_it` (before `test_br10_one_order_per_idempotency_key`), add:

```python
def test_br09_the_order_header_never_changes(cur, customer, product_id, violates):
    order_id = place_order(cur, customer, [(product_id("APL-AP4"), 1, "189.00")])    # Click & Collect at store 1
    other = make_customer(cur, "other.customer@example.com", with_address=False)
    for column, value in [("order_id", 999999),
                          ("customer_id", other),
                          ("idempotency_key", str(uuid.uuid4())),
                          ("fulfilment_method", "delivery"),
                          ("fulfilling_store_id", 2),     # retail, so BR06 (whose trigger fires first) agrees
                          ("delivery_address_no", 1),
                          ("placed_at", datetime(2026, 1, 1, tzinfo=timezone.utc))]:
        update = sql.SQL("UPDATE customer_order SET {} = %s WHERE order_id = %s").format(sql.Identifier(column))
        violates("br09_order_header_immutable", update, (value, order_id))
    pay(cur, order_id)                                    # the status is the one thing that moves


def test_br09_history_is_append_only(collected_order, violates):
    violates("br09_history_append_only",
             "UPDATE order_status_history SET changed_by_staff_id = NULL WHERE order_id = %s", (collected_order,))
    violates("br09_history_append_only", "DELETE FROM order_status_history WHERE order_id = %s", (collected_order,))
    violates("br09_history_append_only", "TRUNCATE order_status_history")     # row triggers don't fire on TRUNCATE
```

(`placed_at` is set to a fixed date on purpose. `SET placed_at = now()` in the transaction that created the order is *not* a change, because `now()` is the transaction start, which is also `placed_at`'s default.)

After `test_br19_one_identity_per_person`, before the `# ---------------------------------------------------------------- views` line, add:

```python
# ---------------------------------------------------------------- BR20: paid in full

CHARGE = "INSERT INTO payment (order_id, kind, amount) VALUES (%s, 'charge', %s)"
PAY = "UPDATE customer_order SET status = 'paid' WHERE order_id = %s"


def test_br20_an_order_is_paid_only_when_its_charge_equals_the_total(cur, customer, product_id, violates):
    order_id = place_order(cur, customer, [(product_id("APL-AP4"), 2, "189.00"),
                                           (product_id("JBL-FLIP7-B"), 1, "149.95")])     # total 527.95
    violates("br20_paid_in_full", PAY, (order_id,))                       # nothing charged
    for wrong in ("378.00", "600.00"):                                    # one line short; too much
        cur.execute("SAVEPOINT wrong_charge")
        cur.execute(CHARGE, (order_id, wrong))
        violates("br20_paid_in_full", PAY, (order_id,))
        cur.execute("ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT wrong_charge")
    cur.execute(CHARGE, (order_id, "527.95"))
    cur.execute(PAY, (order_id,))
    cur.execute("SELECT status FROM customer_order WHERE order_id = %s", (order_id,))
    assert cur.fetchone()["status"] == "paid"


def test_br20_an_order_without_lines_cannot_be_paid(cur, customer, violates):
    order_id = place_order(cur, customer, [])          # 0.00 due and 0.00 charged, but nothing was bought
    violates("br20_paid_in_full", PAY, (order_id,))


def test_br20_a_zero_total_order_is_paid_without_a_charge(cur, customer, product_id):
    # A line may cost 0.00 (money_aud allows it) but a charge may not (payment_amount_positive), so when nothing
    # is due, "charged in full" means no charge at all.
    order_id = place_order(cur, customer, [(product_id("APL-AP4"), 1, "0.00")])
    cur.execute(PAY, (order_id,))
    cur.execute("SELECT status FROM customer_order WHERE order_id = %s", (order_id,))
    assert cur.fetchone()["status"] == "paid"


def test_br20_a_charge_is_taken_only_while_the_order_is_placed(cur, customer, product_id, violates):
    paid = place_order(cur, customer, [(product_id("APL-AP4"), 1, "0.00")])
    cur.execute(PAY, (paid,))                  # paid with no charge row, so a new charge can't be a second one
    violates("br20_charge_while_placed", CHARGE, (paid, "1.00"))
    cancelled = place_order(cur, customer, [(product_id("JBL-FLIP7-B"), 1, "149.95")])
    advance(cur, cancelled, "cancelled")
    violates("br20_charge_while_placed", CHARGE, (cancelled, "149.95"))
    violates("payment_order_fk", CHARGE, (-1, "1.00"))                    # no such order: the foreign key says so


def test_br20_at_most_one_charge_per_order(cur, customer, product_id, violates):
    order_id = place_order(cur, customer, [(product_id("APL-AP4"), 1, "189.00")])
    cur.execute(CHARGE, (order_id, "189.00"))
    violates("br20_one_charge_per_order", CHARGE, (order_id, "189.00"))   # still placed: only the index stops it
```

  - `test_br09_history_is_append_only`

  - `test_br18_the_log_is_append_only` (at its new TRUNCATE line)
  - `test_br20_an_order_is_paid_only_when_its_charge_equals_the_total`
  - `test_br20_an_order_without_lines_cannot_be_paid`
  - `test_br20_a_charge_is_taken_only_while_the_order_is_placed`
  - `test_br20_at_most_one_charge_per_order`
- `test_br09_the_order_header_never_changes` fails with `assert 'order_line_order_fk' == 'br09_order_header_immutable'`. Today only the foreign key stops an `order_id` change.

- [ ] **Step 4: One charge per order — the partial unique index (`sql/01_tables.sql`)**

At the end of the file, after the closing `);` of `CREATE TABLE payment (…)`, add:

```sql
-- BR20: an order is charged at most once (the rest of BR20 is in 02_rules.sql). A partial unique index is
-- UNIQUE over only the rows its WHERE matches, so it doesn't limit an order's refunds. Beyond Week 10
-- (optional, plan T-8): without it BR20's paid-in-full trigger would still refuse to mark an over-charged
-- order paid; the index refuses the second charge itself.
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX br20_one_charge_per_order ON payment (order_id) WHERE kind = 'charge';
```

The name the client sees for a unique-index violation is the index name: SQLSTATE 23505, `e.diag.constraint_name = 'br20_one_charge_per_order'`. So `violates()` and the API's `IntegrityError` → 409 mapping work unchanged.

- [ ] **Step 5: Widen BR08 (`sql/02_rules.sql`)**

Replace the whole `BR08` section, from `-- ---------------------------------------------------------------- BR08` through `FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION br08_block_unit_price_change();`, with:

```sql
-- ---------------------------------------------------------------- BR08
-- An order line is a purchase record. Lines are added only while their order is placed (once it is paid,
-- BR20 has checked the charge against exactly these lines), and a line is never changed or deleted, not
-- even while its order is placed. One function raises both BR08 codes, so a unit-price change is always
-- reported as br08_unit_price_immutable, whatever else the UPDATE does. TRUNCATE skips row triggers: see
-- the append-only section at the end of this file.

CREATE FUNCTION br08_guard_order_line() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
DECLARE
    parent_status order_status;
BEGIN
    IF TG_OP = 'INSERT' THEN
        -- Locking the order serialises this INSERT with placed → paid, which locks the same row: either
        -- the move waits and BR20 counts this line, or this INSERT waits and then sees the order paid.
        SELECT status INTO parent_status FROM customer_order WHERE order_id = NEW.order_id
        FOR UPDATE;
        IF parent_status <> 'placed' THEN             -- no such order (NULL): order_line_order_fk says so
            RAISE EXCEPTION 'Order % is %: lines can only be added while it is placed', NEW.order_id, parent_status
                USING ERRCODE = 'check_violation', CONSTRAINT = 'br08_order_line_immutable';
        END IF;
        RETURN NEW;
    END IF;

    IF TG_OP = 'UPDATE' THEN
        IF NEW.unit_price IS DISTINCT FROM OLD.unit_price THEN
            RAISE EXCEPTION 'An order line keeps the price it was sold at'
                USING ERRCODE = 'check_violation', CONSTRAINT = 'br08_unit_price_immutable';
        END IF;
        IF (NEW.order_id, NEW.line_no, NEW.product_id, NEW.quantity)
           IS NOT DISTINCT FROM (OLD.order_id, OLD.line_no, OLD.product_id, OLD.quantity) THEN
            RETURN NEW;                               -- nothing changes
        END IF;
    END IF;

    RAISE EXCEPTION 'An order line is a purchase record: it can''t be changed or deleted'
        USING ERRCODE = 'check_violation', CONSTRAINT = 'br08_order_line_immutable';
END $$;

CREATE TRIGGER br08_order_line_immutable
    BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE OR DELETE ON order_line
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION br08_guard_order_line();
```

How it works:
- **Missing order.** If the order doesn't exist, `parent_status` is NULL. `NULL <> 'placed'` is not true, so the trigger lets the row through and `order_line_order_fk` reports it.
- **Names kept.** Main's `br08_block_unit_price_change()` and its trigger `br08_unit_price_immutable` are gone. A price change still raises `br08_unit_price_immutable`, so `test_br08_unit_price_never_changes` and the API's error code are unchanged.
- **Row locks.** `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` on `order_line`, as BR12's trigger does, fires no trigger at all.

- [ ] **Step 6: The order header never changes (BR09, `sql/02_rules.sql`)**

In the `BR09` section, after `CREATE TRIGGER br09_log_status_change … FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION br09_log_status_change();` and before `-- ---------------------------------------------------------------- BR11`, add:

```sql
-- Only an order's status moves. Who ordered, how and where it is fulfilled, the delivery address, the
-- retry key and when it was placed are fixed, so an UPDATE can't re-point an order at another customer,
-- store or address. BEFORE UPDATE triggers fire in name order, so this one runs before
-- br09_order_status_transition and br20_paid_in_full.
CREATE FUNCTION br09_block_order_header_change() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
BEGIN
    IF (NEW.order_id, NEW.customer_id, NEW.idempotency_key, NEW.fulfilment_method,
        NEW.fulfilling_store_id, NEW.delivery_address_no, NEW.placed_at)
       IS DISTINCT FROM
       (OLD.order_id, OLD.customer_id, OLD.idempotency_key, OLD.fulfilment_method,
        OLD.fulfilling_store_id, OLD.delivery_address_no, OLD.placed_at) THEN
        RAISE EXCEPTION 'Only an order''s status can change: who, how, where and when it was placed are fixed'
            USING ERRCODE = 'check_violation', CONSTRAINT = 'br09_order_header_immutable';
    END IF;
    RETURN NEW;
END $$;

CREATE TRIGGER br09_order_header_immutable
    BEFORE UPDATE ON customer_order
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION br09_block_order_header_change();
```

- `IS DISTINCT FROM` on the two row values treats two NULLs as equal. That matters because `delivery_address_no` is NULL for Click & Collect.
- `br06_collect_at_retail_store` still answers first when a Click & Collect order is moved to the warehouse (`br06…` sorts before `br09…`). Both rules refuse that change.

- [ ] **Step 7: BR20 and the append-only records (`sql/02_rules.sql`)**

At the end of the file, after `CREATE TRIGGER br18_adjustment_append_only … FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION br18_block_adjustment_change();`, add:

```sql
-- ---------------------------------------------------------------- BR20
-- An order is paid only when it has lines and its charge is exactly its total. That is an assertion over
-- three tables (customer_order, order_line, payment), which PostgreSQL doesn't support, so triggers
-- emulate it (as for BR12). It is checked on the one change where it starts to matter, placed → paid, and
-- stays true afterwards because nothing it reads can change once the order is paid: lines are added only
-- while it is placed (BR08), it is charged only while placed and at most once (below, and the partial
-- unique index br20_one_charge_per_order in 01_tables.sql), and payments are never updated or deleted
-- (the append-only section at the end of this file).
-- The order-row locks rely on READ COMMITTED, the project's isolation level: once a statement has waited
-- for the lock, the trigger's next query takes a fresh snapshot and sees what the other transaction
-- committed. (At REPEATABLE READ it would read its older snapshot and could miss a new line.)

-- A charge is taken only while the order is placed. Locking the order serialises this INSERT with
-- placed → paid, which locks the same row, so the two can't both act on a stale status.
CREATE FUNCTION br20_check_charge() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
DECLARE
    parent_status order_status;
BEGIN
    IF NEW.kind = 'charge' THEN
        SELECT status INTO parent_status FROM customer_order WHERE order_id = NEW.order_id
        FOR UPDATE;
        IF parent_status <> 'placed' THEN             -- no such order (NULL): payment_order_fk says so
            RAISE EXCEPTION 'Order % is %: it can only be charged while it is placed', NEW.order_id, parent_status
                USING ERRCODE = 'check_violation', CONSTRAINT = 'br20_charge_while_placed';
        END IF;
    END IF;
    RETURN NEW;
END $$;

CREATE TRIGGER br20_charge_while_placed
    BEFORE INSERT ON payment
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION br20_check_charge();

-- v_order_total is created in 03_views.sql. PL/pgSQL looks names up when the function first runs, not
-- when it is created, so the file order doesn't matter.
CREATE FUNCTION br20_check_paid_in_full() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
DECLARE
    lines   BIGINT;
    due     NUMERIC(10,2);
    charged NUMERIC(10,2);
BEGIN
    IF OLD.status = 'placed' AND NEW.status = 'paid' THEN
        SELECT line_count, total INTO lines, due
        FROM v_order_total
        WHERE order_id = NEW.order_id;

        SELECT COALESCE(sum(amount), 0) INTO charged
        FROM payment
        WHERE order_id = NEW.order_id AND kind = 'charge';

        IF lines = 0 OR charged <> due THEN
            RAISE EXCEPTION 'Order % can''t be paid: % line(s), % due, % charged', NEW.order_id, lines, due, charged
                USING ERRCODE = 'check_violation', CONSTRAINT = 'br20_paid_in_full';
        END IF;
    END IF;
    RETURN NEW;
END $$;

-- Named br20_…, so it fires after br09_order_header_immutable and br09_order_status_transition: an
-- illegal move is reported as BR09 before BR20 looks at the money.
CREATE TRIGGER br20_paid_in_full
    BEFORE UPDATE OF status ON customer_order
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION br20_check_paid_in_full();

-- ---------------------------------------------------------------- append-only records
-- The payment ledger (BR14), the status history (BR09), the stock adjustment log (BR18) and order lines
-- (BR08) are records: a mistake is corrected by a new row, never by editing or deleting one. One function
-- serves every trigger below; the rule it reports is the trigger's argument (TG_ARGV[0]).
-- Row triggers don't fire on TRUNCATE, so each of the four tables also gets a statement-level BEFORE
-- TRUNCATE trigger; TRUNCATE … CASCADE fires those of every table it reaches, so emptying customer_order
-- that way is refused too. (BR08's and BR18's row triggers are in their own sections above.)

CREATE FUNCTION block_append_only_change() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
BEGIN
    RAISE EXCEPTION '% rows are a permanent record: % is not allowed', TG_TABLE_NAME, TG_OP
        USING ERRCODE = 'check_violation', CONSTRAINT = TG_ARGV[0];
END $$;

CREATE TRIGGER br14_payment_append_only
    BEFORE UPDATE OR DELETE ON payment
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION block_append_only_change('br14_payment_append_only');

CREATE TRIGGER br09_history_append_only
    BEFORE UPDATE OR DELETE ON order_status_history
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION block_append_only_change('br09_history_append_only');

CREATE TRIGGER br14_payment_append_only_truncate
    BEFORE TRUNCATE ON payment
    FOR EACH STATEMENT EXECUTE FUNCTION block_append_only_change('br14_payment_append_only');

CREATE TRIGGER br09_history_append_only_truncate
    BEFORE TRUNCATE ON order_status_history
    FOR EACH STATEMENT EXECUTE FUNCTION block_append_only_change('br09_history_append_only');

CREATE TRIGGER br18_adjustment_append_only_truncate
    BEFORE TRUNCATE ON stock_adjustment
    FOR EACH STATEMENT EXECUTE FUNCTION block_append_only_change('br18_adjustment_append_only');

CREATE TRIGGER br08_order_line_immutable_truncate
    BEFORE TRUNCATE ON order_line
    FOR EACH STATEMENT EXECUTE FUNCTION block_append_only_change('br08_order_line_immutable');
```

Notes for the report:
- **Kept from main.** Main's BR18 row trigger and `br18_block_adjustment_change()` stay as they are; only the new TRUNCATE trigger uses the shared function.
- **TRUNCATE.** `TRUNCATE order_line` without CASCADE is refused before any trigger runs: SQLSTATE `0A000`, because `return_line` references `order_line`. That is why the test uses CASCADE.
- **Deleting orders.** Report §3 should say that orders with lines or history can't be deleted any more, so the `ON DELETE CASCADE`s on `order_line` and `order_status_history` can never fire.

```python
    order_id = place_order(cur, customer, [])            # accepted for now: an order's lines come after it...
```

The second `SET CONSTRAINTS` works because rolling back to `violates()`'s savepoint puts the failed check back in the queue. It then runs again and passes once the line exists.

(c) Immediately before `def test_br10_one_order_per_idempotency_key(`, i.e. after the last BR09 test:

```python
HISTORY = "INSERT INTO order_status_history (order_id, seq, from_status, to_status) VALUES (%s, %s, %s, %s)"


def test_br09_only_the_status_log_writes_history(cur, customer, product_id, violates):
    order_id = place_order(cur, customer, [(product_id("APL-AP4"), 1, "189.00")])
    # A well-formed row, written directly: refused, so the timeline can't disagree with the order's status.
    violates("br09_history_written_by_trigger", HISTORY, (order_id, 2, "placed", "paid"))
    pay(cur, order_id)                                   # the status change writes row 2 itself
    cur.execute("SELECT seq, from_status, to_status FROM order_status_history WHERE order_id = %s ORDER BY seq",
                (order_id,))
    assert [tuple(row.values()) for row in cur.fetchall()] == [(1, None, "placed"), (2, "placed", "paid")]


def test_br09_history_rows_are_well_formed(cur, customer, product_id, violates):
    order_id = place_order(cur, customer, [(product_id("APL-AP4"), 1, "189.00")])     # row 1: NULL → placed
    # br09_history_written_by_trigger refuses a direct INSERT before any CHECK is reached, so it is switched off
    # for this transaction only (ALTER TABLE is transactional: the test's rollback would restore it too).
    cur.execute("ALTER TABLE order_status_history DISABLE TRIGGER br09_history_written_by_trigger")
    violates("br09_history_first_row_is_creation", HISTORY, (order_id, 2, None, "paid"))       # later rows have a from
    violates("br09_history_first_row_is_creation", HISTORY, (order_id, 1, "placed", "paid"))   # row 1 has none
    violates("br09_history_status_changes", HISTORY, (order_id, 2, "placed", "placed"))         # not a change
    cur.execute(HISTORY, (order_id, 2, "placed", "paid"))                                       # well formed: accepted
    cur.execute("ALTER TABLE order_status_history ENABLE TRIGGER br09_history_written_by_trigger")
    violates("br09_history_written_by_trigger", HISTORY, (order_id, 3, "paid", "picking"))      # and the guard is back
```

Three things the BR09 tests rely on:
- The `(order_id, 1, …)` row reports the CHECK, not `order_status_history_pk`, because CHECKs run before the primary-key index is checked.
- `ALTER TABLE order_status_history` is allowed even though the order's deferred `br07_order_has_lines` check is queued, because that event belongs to `customer_order`. `ALTER TABLE customer_order` would fail here with 55006.
- The table lock taken by `DISABLE TRIGGER` lasts until the test's rollback. pytest runs one test at a time, so no other test waits on it.

- [ ] **Step 3: Create `tests/test_checkout_transactions.py` with the BR06 race test**

```python
"""Order rules when transactions race (T1, plan §5). Each "request" here is what the API does: its own
connection and one transaction, committed or rolled back, released at the same moment as the others
(run_at_once). These tests commit, so each one works on rows it creates itself and puts back anything else
it changes.
"""
import time
import uuid

import psycopg2

from tests.support import connect, run_at_once

ROBINA = 12             # a retail store that no other test orders from
HOLD = 0.3              # seconds a racing transaction stays open after its check, so the two always overlap


def test_br06_a_store_type_change_and_a_collect_order_race_has_one_winner():
    """A Click & Collect order at Robina, and Robina becoming a warehouse, at the same moment. Each BR06 check
    passes on what is committed, so without a lock both would commit: a Click & Collect order at a warehouse.
    The order's trigger reads the store FOR SHARE, which the store's UPDATE must wait for (and the other way
    round), so whichever comes second waits for the first to commit, then sees it and is refused."""
    setup = connect()
    with setup, setup.cursor() as cur:
        cur.execute("""SELECT count(*) AS n FROM customer_order
                       WHERE fulfilling_store_id = %s AND fulfilment_method = 'click_collect'""", (ROBINA,))
        assert cur.fetchone()["n"] == 0, "Robina already has a Click & Collect order, so there is no race"
        cur.execute("INSERT INTO customer (email, full_name) VALUES (%s, 'Race Tester') RETURNING customer_id",
                    (f"race.{uuid.uuid4().hex[:10]}@example.com",))
        customer_id = cur.fetchone()["customer_id"]
        cur.execute("SELECT product_id FROM product WHERE sku = 'APL-AP4'")
        airpods = cur.fetchone()["product_id"]
    setup.close()

    def place_collect_order():
        conn = connect()
        try:
            with conn, conn.cursor() as cur:
                cur.execute("SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '10s'")   # a lock regression fails the test, never hangs it
                cur.execute("""INSERT INTO customer_order (customer_id, idempotency_key, fulfilment_method,
                                                           fulfilling_store_id)
                               VALUES (%s, gen_random_uuid(), 'click_collect', %s)
                               RETURNING order_id""", (customer_id, ROBINA))
                cur.execute("""INSERT INTO order_line (order_id, line_no, product_id, quantity, unit_price)
                               VALUES (%s, 1, %s, 1, 189.00)""", (cur.fetchone()["order_id"], airpods))
                time.sleep(HOLD)
            return "order"
        finally:
            conn.close()

    def make_robina_a_warehouse():
        conn = connect()
        try:
            with conn, conn.cursor() as cur:
                cur.execute("SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '10s'")
                cur.execute("UPDATE store SET store_type = 'warehouse' WHERE store_id = %s", (ROBINA,))
                time.sleep(HOLD)
            return "warehouse"
        finally:
            conn.close()

    jobs = [place_collect_order, make_robina_a_warehouse]
    try:
        results = run_at_once(2, lambda: jobs.pop()())      # each thread takes one of the two jobs
        winners = [result for result in results if isinstance(result, str)]
        refused = [result for result in results if isinstance(result, psycopg2.Error)]
        assert (len(winners), len(refused)) == (1, 1), results
        assert refused[0].diag.constraint_name == "br06_collect_at_retail_store"

        check = connect()
        with check, check.cursor() as cur:
            cur.execute("SELECT store_type FROM store WHERE store_id = %s", (ROBINA,))
            store_type = cur.fetchone()["store_type"]
            cur.execute("SELECT count(*) AS n FROM customer_order WHERE customer_id = %s", (customer_id,))
            orders = cur.fetchone()["n"]
        check.close()
        # Exactly the winner's work was committed.
        assert (store_type, orders) == {"order": ("retail", 1), "warehouse": ("warehouse", 0)}[winners[0]]
    finally:
        cleanup = connect()
        with cleanup, cleanup.cursor() as cur:
            # Robina goes back to retail (always allowed: BR06 only stops a store *leaving* retail).
            cur.execute("UPDATE store SET store_type = 'retail' WHERE store_id = %s", (ROBINA,))
            # The customer goes too, unless the order won: orders are purchase records that are never deleted
            # (BR08, BR09), so a winning order stays. The test database is rebuilt on every run.
            cur.execute("""DELETE FROM customer c
                           WHERE c.customer_id = %s
                             AND NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM customer_order o WHERE o.customer_id = c.customer_id)""",
                        (customer_id,))
        cleanup.close()


COLLECT_ORDER = """
    INSERT INTO customer_order (customer_id, idempotency_key, fulfilment_method, fulfilling_store_id)
    SELECT customer_id, gen_random_uuid(), 'click_collect', 2 FROM customer ORDER BY customer_id LIMIT 1
"""


def test_collect_orders_at_one_store_do_not_wait_for_each_other():
    """BR06's order-side check reads the store FOR SHARE. Shared locks don't conflict with each other, so two
    Click & Collect orders at one store never queue behind each other; only a store-type change waits. Were
    the lock FOR UPDATE, the second insert would wait and hit the lock timeout. Nothing is committed."""
    held, other = connect(), connect()
    try:
        with held.cursor() as cur:
            cur.execute(COLLECT_ORDER)                  # keeps its lock on store 2 until the rollback below
        with other.cursor() as cur:
            cur.execute("SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '2s'")
            cur.execute(COLLECT_ORDER)                  # must not wait for the open transaction
    finally:
        held.rollback()
        other.rollback()
        held.close()
        other.close()
```

Why the tests are written this way:
- **The shared-lock test is a regression guard, not a fail-first test.** It already passes before this task (there is no store lock at all yet) and must keep passing after it. It is what pins `FOR SHARE` rather than `FOR UPDATE`: with `FOR UPDATE` the second insert hits the 2 s lock timeout (`LockNotAvailable`). The orders it inserts are rolled back, so the deferred `br07_order_has_lines` never runs for them.
- **A seeded store, not a new one.** A committed new store would break `test_api.py`'s `len(stores) == 13` if that test ran later. It would also leave behind a retail store with no stock, which would distort queries over every store.
- **Cleanup.** Robina is always put back to retail, and the test customer is deleted when no order was committed. A winning order can't be deleted (Task 1 makes lines and history permanent), so it stays until the next test run rebuilds the database.
- **`HOLD`.** With the lock in place, the test always has exactly one winner whatever the timing. The 0.3 s hold only makes sure the two transactions overlap, so that the test fails if the lock is missing.

- [ ] **Step 5: Add the two history CHECKs to `sql/01_tables.sql`**

At the end of `CREATE TABLE order_status_history`, replace

```sql
    CONSTRAINT order_status_history_staff_fk FOREIGN KEY (changed_by_staff_id) REFERENCES staff (staff_id)
);
```

with

```sql
    CONSTRAINT order_status_history_staff_fk FOREIGN KEY (changed_by_staff_id) REFERENCES staff (staff_id),
    -- BR09: row 1 records the order's creation and is the only row without a from_status, and every row
    -- records a real change.
    CONSTRAINT br09_history_first_row_is_creation CHECK ((seq = 1) = (from_status IS NULL)),
    CONSTRAINT br09_history_status_changes CHECK (from_status IS DISTINCT FROM to_status)
);
```

`(seq = 1) = (from_status IS NULL)` compares two booleans. It makes the "creation row" and "row 1" the same thing in both directions. `IS DISTINCT FROM` treats NULL as a value, so the creation row (NULL → placed) passes, and a row that changes nothing (placed → placed) fails.

- [ ] **Step 6: BR06 from both sides, in `sql/02_rules.sql`**

Why a lock is needed: the order's trigger reads `store.store_type`, and the store's new trigger reads `customer_order`. Under READ COMMITTED each sees only committed rows, so a Click & Collect order and a retail → warehouse change that overlap would both pass and both commit. The FK's `FOR KEY SHARE` on the store doesn't help, because it doesn't conflict with an UPDATE of a non-key column such as `store_type`. `FOR SHARE` does conflict with that UPDATE, so whichever transaction comes second waits for the first. Two `FOR SHARE` locks are compatible, so orders at the same store never wait for each other.

Verified while planning:
- With a Click & Collect order open, a non-key UPDATE of its store waits.
- With a delivery order open, it doesn't wait (only `FOR KEY SHARE` is held).
- Two Click & Collect orders at one store don't block each other.
- Both race directions end with `br06_collect_at_retail_store` for the second transaction.

First, the file's header comment. Replace

```sql
-- Where two concurrent transactions could both pass a check, the trigger first locks a parent row
-- (SELECT … FOR UPDATE) so the checks run one after the other.
```

with

```sql
-- Where two concurrent transactions could both pass a check, the trigger first locks a row they both
-- need (SELECT … FOR UPDATE, or FOR SHARE when only a change to that row has to wait, as in BR06), so
-- the checks run one after the other.
```

Then replace the whole BR06 section. This is main's text, which Task 1 does not change:

```sql
-- ---------------------------------------------------------------- BR06
-- Click & Collect orders are collected at a retail store. store_type lives in another table.

CREATE FUNCTION br06_check_collect_store() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
BEGIN
    IF NEW.fulfilment_method = 'click_collect'
       AND (SELECT store_type FROM store WHERE store_id = NEW.fulfilling_store_id) <> 'retail' THEN
        RAISE EXCEPTION 'Click & Collect orders must be collected from a retail store'
            USING ERRCODE = 'check_violation', CONSTRAINT = 'br06_collect_at_retail_store';
    END IF;
    RETURN NEW;
END $$;

CREATE TRIGGER br06_collect_at_retail_store
    BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE OF fulfilment_method, fulfilling_store_id ON customer_order
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION br06_check_collect_store();
```

with

```sql
-- ---------------------------------------------------------------- BR06
-- Click & Collect orders are collected at a retail store. store_type lives in another table, so the rule
-- has a guard on each side, as BR15 does: an order checks its store, and a store that has Click & Collect
-- orders can't stop being retail. Both raise br06_collect_at_retail_store.

CREATE FUNCTION br06_check_collect_store() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
DECLARE
    collect_store_type TEXT;
BEGIN
    IF NEW.fulfilment_method = 'click_collect' THEN
        -- FOR SHARE: a shared lock on the store row until this transaction ends. A change to the store
        -- waits for this order to commit, and an order that arrives while a change is uncommitted waits
        -- for it, then reads the new type. Orders at the same store don't wait for each other: shared
        -- locks are compatible.
        SELECT store_type INTO collect_store_type
        FROM store
        WHERE store_id = NEW.fulfilling_store_id
        FOR SHARE;
        IF collect_store_type <> 'retail' THEN        -- no such store (NULL): customer_order_store_fk says so
            RAISE EXCEPTION 'Click & Collect orders must be collected from a retail store'
                USING ERRCODE = 'check_violation', CONSTRAINT = 'br06_collect_at_retail_store';
        END IF;
    END IF;
    RETURN NEW;
END $$;

CREATE TRIGGER br06_collect_at_retail_store
    BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE OF fulfilment_method, fulfilling_store_id ON customer_order
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION br06_check_collect_store();

-- The store side needs no lock of its own: its UPDATE locks the store row, so it first waits for any order
-- holding FOR SHARE on it; then this query, which takes a fresh snapshot under READ COMMITTED, sees that
-- order.
CREATE FUNCTION br06_check_store_type() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
BEGIN
    IF NEW.store_type <> 'retail'
       AND EXISTS (SELECT 1
                   FROM customer_order
                   WHERE fulfilling_store_id = NEW.store_id
                     AND fulfilment_method = 'click_collect') THEN
        RAISE EXCEPTION 'Store % has Click & Collect orders, so it must stay a retail store', NEW.store_id
            USING ERRCODE = 'check_violation', CONSTRAINT = 'br06_collect_at_retail_store';
    END IF;
    RETURN NEW;
END $$;

CREATE TRIGGER br06_store_stays_retail
    BEFORE UPDATE OF store_type ON store
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION br06_check_store_type();
```

The variable is called `collect_store_type`, not `store_type`, because a PL/pgSQL variable named like the column would make `SELECT store_type …` ambiguous. The lookup moved from main's single `IF … AND (SELECT …)` into its own statement inside the `IF`. That way the lock is only ever taken for Click & Collect orders, without relying on how `AND` is evaluated.

- [ ] **Step 7: Add the BR07 section to `sql/02_rules.sql`**

Insert immediately before the line `-- ---------------------------------------------------------------- BR08` (the start of Task 1's BR08 section):

```sql
-- ---------------------------------------------------------------- BR07
-- An order is for products that are still on sale, and has at least one line. (A positive quantity and
-- each product once per order: CHECK and UNIQUE in 01_tables.sql.)

-- No lock on the product: a product retired while a checkout is running can still be sold by that
-- checkout, which read it while it was on sale. The rule holds at the moment of sale.
CREATE FUNCTION br07_check_product_on_sale() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
BEGIN
    -- A product that doesn't exist matches nothing here: order_line_product_fk says so.
    IF EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM product WHERE product_id = NEW.product_id AND NOT is_active) THEN
        RAISE EXCEPTION 'Product % is no longer sold', NEW.product_id
            USING ERRCODE = 'check_violation', CONSTRAINT = 'br07_product_on_sale';
    END IF;
    RETURN NEW;
END $$;

CREATE TRIGGER br07_product_on_sale
    BEFORE INSERT ON order_line
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION br07_check_product_on_sale();

-- An order's lines are inserted after the order (they refer to it), so "has a line" can't be checked when
-- the order row goes in. A constraint trigger declared DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED runs at COMMIT, once
-- the whole order is in; lines are never deleted (BR08), so it stays true. Beyond Week 10 (optional, plan
-- T-8): without it, BR20 still refuses to mark an order without lines paid.
CREATE FUNCTION br07_check_order_has_lines() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
BEGIN
    IF NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM order_line WHERE order_id = NEW.order_id) THEN
        RAISE EXCEPTION 'Order % has no lines: an order is for at least one product', NEW.order_id
            USING ERRCODE = 'check_violation', CONSTRAINT = 'br07_order_has_lines';
    END IF;
    RETURN NULL;                                      -- an AFTER trigger's result is ignored
END $$;

CREATE CONSTRAINT TRIGGER br07_order_has_lines
    AFTER INSERT ON customer_order
    DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION br07_check_order_has_lines();
```

How the deferred check behaves (all verified while planning):
- Each inserted order queues one check, which runs at COMMIT. A real `COMMIT` of an order without lines raises `CheckViolation` with `br07_order_has_lines`, and nothing is kept. Through the API that becomes a 409, because `get_cursor` commits before the response is sent.
- T1's `INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` queues no check for a row it skips.
- While a check is queued, `ALTER TABLE customer_order` and `TRUNCATE customer_order` in the same transaction fail with 55006. Task 1's `TRUNCATE order_line CASCADE`, `order_status_history`, `payment` and `stock_adjustment` checks still report their rule names.

- [ ] **Step 8: Add the history-writer guard at the end of the BR09 section of `sql/02_rules.sql`**

Insert immediately before the line `-- ---------------------------------------------------------------- BR11`, i.e. after Task 1's `br09_order_header_immutable` trigger:

```sql
-- Only br09_log_status_change writes the history. Inside this trigger pg_trigger_depth() is 1 when someone
-- INSERTs directly, and 2 or more when the INSERT comes from the log trigger (a trigger firing a trigger).
-- Beyond Week 10 (optional, plan T-8): it can tell only that *a* trigger is writing, not which one; the two
-- CHECKs on order_status_history (01_tables.sql) hold whoever writes.
CREATE FUNCTION br09_check_history_writer() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
BEGIN
    IF pg_trigger_depth() < 2 THEN
        RAISE EXCEPTION 'Order status history is written by the status-change trigger, not directly'
            USING ERRCODE = 'check_violation', CONSTRAINT = 'br09_history_written_by_trigger';
    END IF;
    RETURN NEW;
END $$;

CREATE TRIGGER br09_history_written_by_trigger
    BEFORE INSERT ON order_status_history
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION br09_check_history_writer();
```

A `DO` block or a function called directly runs at depth 0, so this guard sees depth 1 and refuses the insert (verified).

- [ ] **Step 9: Add the BR17 section to `sql/02_rules.sql`**

Insert immediately before the line `-- ---------------------------------------------------------------- BR18`:

```sql
-- ---------------------------------------------------------------- BR17
-- A delivery goes to one of the customer's current addresses. Whose address it is: the composite FK
-- br17_order_address_is_customers (01_tables.sql). A retired address stays for the orders that used it
-- (BR16), but no new order may use it. No lock, as for BR07's products: the rule holds when the order is
-- placed.

CREATE FUNCTION br17_check_address_active() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
BEGIN
    -- A missing address, or another customer's, matches nothing here: the composite FK says so. A Click &
    -- Collect order with an address is left to the CHECK br06_order_address_matches_method.
    IF NEW.fulfilment_method = 'delivery'
       AND EXISTS (SELECT 1
                   FROM customer_address
                   WHERE customer_id = NEW.customer_id
                     AND address_no = NEW.delivery_address_no
                     AND NOT is_active) THEN
        RAISE EXCEPTION 'Address % has been retired: choose a current address', NEW.delivery_address_no
            USING ERRCODE = 'check_violation', CONSTRAINT = 'br17_order_address_active';
    END IF;
    RETURN NEW;
END $$;

CREATE TRIGGER br17_order_address_active
    BEFORE INSERT ON customer_order
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION br17_check_address_active();
```

BEFORE INSERT triggers fire even when `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` then skips the row. So a checkout retry made after its address was retired would get `br17_order_address_active` at T1's claim step (verified). That is why T1 looks the key up before claiming (spec §3 step 1, Task 7).

  - Task 2: trigger `br09_history_written_by_trigger` on `order_status_history` (fn `br09_check_history_writer`; refuses a direct INSERT).
  - Main: `place_order(cur, customer_id, lines, method="click_collect", store_id=1, address_no=None) -> int`, `advance(cur, order_id, *statuses, staff_id=None)`, `request_return(cur, order_id, lines, days_from_now=0) -> int`, fixtures `cur`, `customer`, `product_id`; view `v_order_total(order_id, line_count, total)`.
- Produces:

  - `v_order_payment(order_id int, charged numeric(10,2), refunded numeric(10,2), balance numeric(10,2))`: one row per order; `balance = charged - refunded`; zero reads `0.00`.
  - `v_order_timeline(order_id int, seq int, from_status order_status, to_status order_status, changed_at timestamptz, changed_by_staff_id int, changed_by_name text)`: no ORDER BY inside; readers sort by `seq`.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing tests**

Insert after `test_current_price_includes_the_running_promotion` (two blank lines before each top-level definition, as in the rest of the file). The summary test builds its own order instead of using `collected_order`, because its `ALTER TABLE` has to run before the history rows are written, and fixtures run before the test body.

```python
def test_order_summary_has_the_total_store_and_last_update(cur, customer, product_id):
    # now() is fixed for a whole transaction, so every history row here would get the same changed_at and
    # the newest change couldn't be told from the first. For this test, history rows record the real time
    # (clock_timestamp()); the ALTER is rolled back with everything else.
    cur.execute("ALTER TABLE order_status_history ALTER COLUMN changed_at SET DEFAULT clock_timestamp()")
    order_id = place_order(cur, customer, [(product_id("APL-AP4"), 2, "189.00"),
                                           (product_id("JBL-FLIP7-B"), 1, "149.95")])
    pay(cur, order_id)
    advance(cur, order_id, "picking", "ready", "collected", staff_id=14)

    cur.execute("SELECT * FROM v_order_summary WHERE order_id = %s", (order_id,))
    rows = cur.fetchall()
    assert len(rows) == 1                      # its 2 lines and 5 history rows don't multiply the order
    summary = rows[0]
    assert list(summary) == ["order_id", "customer_id", "placed_at", "status", "fulfilment_method",
                             "fulfilling_store_id", "fulfilling_store_name", "total", "line_count",
                             "last_updated_at"]
    assert (summary["customer_id"], summary["status"], summary["fulfilment_method"], summary["fulfilling_store_id"],
            summary["fulfilling_store_name"], str(summary["total"]), summary["line_count"]) \
        == (customer, "collected", "click_collect", 1, "Sydney CBD", "527.95", 2)
    cur.execute("SELECT changed_at FROM order_status_history WHERE order_id = %s ORDER BY seq", (order_id,))
    first, *_, newest = [row["changed_at"] for row in cur.fetchall()]
    assert summary["last_updated_at"] == newest > first


def test_order_payment_balances_charges_and_refunds_without_fan_out(cur, customer, collected_order, product_id):
    def payment_of(order_id):
        cur.execute("SELECT charged, refunded, balance FROM v_order_payment WHERE order_id = %s", (order_id,))
        return [tuple(str(value) for value in row.values()) for row in cur.fetchall()]

    # collected_order: two lines, one charge of 527.95. Nothing refunded yet reads 0.00, not 0.
    assert payment_of(collected_order) == [("527.95", "0.00", "527.95")]
    # Refund one AirPods (line 1) and the Flip 7 (line 2), each on its own received return.
    for line_no, amount in [(1, "189.00"), (2, "149.95")]:
        return_id = request_return(cur, collected_order, [(line_no, 1)])
        cur.execute("""UPDATE return_request SET status = 'received', received_store_id = 1, processed_by_staff_id = 14
                       WHERE return_id = %s""", (return_id,))
        cur.execute("INSERT INTO payment (order_id, kind, amount, return_id) VALUES (%s, 'refund', %s, %s)",
                    (collected_order, amount, return_id))
    # Summed per order before any join, so the two lines don't double the three payments (joining the lines
    # first would report 1055.90 charged and 677.90 refunded).
    assert payment_of(collected_order) == [("527.95", "338.95", "189.00")]
    # An order with no payments yet still has its one row.
    unpaid = place_order(cur, customer, [(product_id("SONY-WH1000XM5-B"), 1, "399.00")])
    assert payment_of(unpaid) == [("0.00", "0.00", "0.00")]
    cur.execute("SELECT (SELECT count(*) FROM v_order_payment) = (SELECT count(*) FROM customer_order) AS one_each")
    assert cur.fetchone()["one_each"]


def test_order_timeline_names_the_staff_in_seq_order(cur, collected_order):
    cur.execute("SELECT * FROM v_order_timeline WHERE order_id = %s ORDER BY seq", (collected_order,))
    rows = cur.fetchall()
    assert list(rows[0]) == ["order_id", "seq", "from_status", "to_status", "changed_at",
                             "changed_by_staff_id", "changed_by_name"]
    # The customer's own moves have no staff member and are still listed (LEFT JOIN): all five rows.
    assert [(r["seq"], r["from_status"], r["to_status"], r["changed_by_staff_id"], r["changed_by_name"])
            for r in rows] == [
        (1, None, "placed", None, None),
        (2, "placed", "paid", None, None),
        (3, "paid", "picking", 14, "Aiden Chen"),
        (4, "picking", "ready", 14, "Aiden Chen"),
        (5, "ready", "collected", 14, "Aiden Chen"),
    ]


# Status and history must agree (BR09, R11). Each query lists the orders that break one condition, over
# every order in the database, including those other test files committed. NOT EXISTS and max(seq) only:
# no window functions.
HISTORY_CHECKS = {
    "status is the newest row's to_status": """
        SELECT o.order_id
        FROM customer_order o
        WHERE NOT EXISTS (
            SELECT 1
            FROM order_status_history h
            WHERE h.order_id = o.order_id
              AND h.to_status = o.status
              AND h.seq = (SELECT max(h2.seq) FROM order_status_history h2 WHERE h2.order_id = o.order_id))
    """,
    "each row starts where the one before ended": """
        SELECT h.order_id
        FROM order_status_history h
        WHERE h.seq > 1
          AND NOT EXISTS (
              SELECT 1
              FROM order_status_history prev
              WHERE prev.order_id = h.order_id
                AND prev.to_status = h.from_status
                AND prev.seq = (SELECT max(p2.seq) FROM order_status_history p2
                                WHERE p2.order_id = h.order_id AND p2.seq < h.seq))
    """,
    "row 1 is NULL -> placed": """
        SELECT o.order_id
        FROM customer_order o
        WHERE NOT EXISTS (
            SELECT 1
            FROM order_status_history h
            WHERE h.order_id = o.order_id AND h.seq = 1 AND h.from_status IS NULL AND h.to_status = 'placed')
    """,
}


def history_breaks(cur) -> dict:
    """{condition: ids of the orders that break it}."""
    found = {}
    for condition, query in HISTORY_CHECKS.items():
        cur.execute(query)
        found[condition] = sorted(row["order_id"] for row in cur.fetchall())
    return found


def test_every_orders_status_is_its_latest_history_entry(cur, customer, collected_order, product_id):
    place_order(cur, customer, [(product_id("SONY-WH1000XM5-B"), 1, "399.00")])           # stays placed
    paid = place_order(cur, customer, [(product_id("JBL-T770NC-B"), 1, "179.95")])
    pay(cur, paid)
    cancelled = place_order(cur, customer, [(product_id("LEN-TABM11-128"), 1, "349.00")])
    advance(cur, cancelled, "cancelled")
    assert history_breaks(cur) == {condition: [] for condition in HISTORY_CHECKS}

    # The checks can fail: forge a sixth row for collected_order that neither matches its status nor starts
    # where row 5 ended. Only BR09's log trigger may write history, so that guard is switched off first
    # (ALTER TABLE is transactional: it is undone with the rest of the test).
    cur.execute("ALTER TABLE order_status_history DISABLE TRIGGER br09_history_written_by_trigger")
    cur.execute("""INSERT INTO order_status_history (order_id, seq, from_status, to_status)
                   VALUES (%s, 6, 'ready', 'cancelled')""", (collected_order,))
    assert history_breaks(cur) == {"status is the newest row's to_status": [collected_order],
                                   "each row starts where the one before ended": [collected_order],
                                   "row 1 is NULL -> placed": []}
```

Both `ALTER TABLE`s touch `order_status_history` only. `ALTER TABLE customer_order` would fail here with 55006 "cannot ALTER TABLE because it has pending trigger events": once a transaction has inserted an order, Task 2's deferred `br07_order_has_lines` has an event queued on that table.

The three view tests fail with `psycopg2.errors.UndefinedTable: relation "v_order_summary" does not exist` (likewise `v_order_payment`, `v_order_timeline`). `test_every_orders_status_is_its_latest_history_entry` already passes: it pins what BR09's log trigger does today, and it is written in this task because it reads the same history the timeline view shows.

- [ ] **Step 3: Add the three views to `sql/03_views.sql`**

Append at the end of the file, after the `v_order_total` view, with one blank line between statements. `v_order_summary` reads `v_order_total`, so it must come after it.

```sql
-- R5: one row per order for "my orders" (and later the store's order queue): where it is fulfilled, its
-- total, and when its status last changed, which is the newest history row (a correlated subquery).
CREATE VIEW v_order_summary AS
SELECT o.order_id,
       o.customer_id,
       o.placed_at,
       o.status,
       o.fulfilment_method,
       o.fulfilling_store_id,
       s.name                            AS fulfilling_store_name,
       t.total,
       t.line_count,
       (SELECT max(h.changed_at)
        FROM order_status_history h
        WHERE h.order_id = o.order_id)   AS last_updated_at
FROM customer_order o
JOIN store s         ON s.store_id = o.fulfilling_store_id
JOIN v_order_total t ON t.order_id = o.order_id;

-- What each order has been charged and refunded, and the balance the shop still holds (charged - refunded).
-- Payments are summed per order in the subquery, *before* the join, so each order meets at most one row
-- and nothing can multiply its payments (summed after a join with its lines, each payment would count once
-- per line). The LEFT JOIN keeps orders with no payments yet; COALESCE makes their sums 0, and the casts
-- keep two decimals, so the API shows 0.00, not 0.
CREATE VIEW v_order_payment AS
SELECT o.order_id,
       CAST(COALESCE(p.charged, 0) AS NUMERIC(10,2))                            AS charged,
       CAST(COALESCE(p.refunded, 0) AS NUMERIC(10,2))                           AS refunded,
       CAST(COALESCE(p.charged, 0) - COALESCE(p.refunded, 0) AS NUMERIC(10,2))  AS balance
FROM customer_order o
LEFT JOIN (SELECT order_id,
                  sum(CASE WHEN kind = 'charge' THEN amount END) AS charged,
                  sum(CASE WHEN kind = 'refund' THEN amount END) AS refunded
           FROM payment
           GROUP BY order_id) p ON p.order_id = o.order_id;

-- R11: an order's status timeline: who, when, from → to. A change made by the customer or the system has
-- no staff member, so this is a LEFT JOIN (an inner join would drop those rows). No ORDER BY in the view:
-- readers sort by seq, because an order's first rows can share one changed_at (one transaction's now()).
CREATE VIEW v_order_timeline AS
SELECT h.order_id,
       h.seq,
       h.from_status,
       h.to_status,
       h.changed_at,
       h.changed_by_staff_id,
       st.full_name AS changed_by_name
FROM order_status_history h
LEFT JOIN staff st ON st.staff_id = h.changed_by_staff_id;
```

Why each piece (for the report and the Q&A):
- `last_updated_at` is a correlated scalar subquery, so the order row is never joined to its history rows. Joining the order to its history and its lines would turn this order into 2 × 5 = 10 rows.
- `v_order_payment` starts from `customer_order` (every order gets one row, even with no payments) and joins an already-aggregated subquery (at most one row per order), so nothing can be counted twice. `sum(CASE WHEN … THEN amount END)` is the same conditional sum BR14's trigger uses (no `FILTER`, no `LATERAL`). Without the casts, `COALESCE(NULL, 0)` is `0` with no decimals and the API would send `"0"`.
- `v_order_timeline` is a LEFT JOIN: with an inner join, `collected_order` keeps only its 3 staff rows out of 5.
- Views carry no ORDER BY. The readers (`get_order`, Task 6) say `ORDER BY seq`, because T1's `placed` and `paid` rows share one `now()`.

```python
"""The catalogue list (R1–R3): the price filter uses the price a product sells for now, a top-level category
includes its subcategories, and one store's stock can come with the list.

The products made here are committed (the API reads through its own connection), so each assertion looks
only at this file's own products, or at seeded rows that no test changes.
"""
from fastapi.testclient import TestClient

from app.main import app
from tests.support import connect, unique_sku

client = TestClient(app)
API = "/api/v1"


def error_of(response):
    return response.status_code, response.json()["error"]["code"]


def a_product(base_price="100.00", sale_price=None, stock=None) -> int:
    """A committed JBL speaker at base_price, on a running promotion at sale_price if one is given, with
    inventory rows only where stock = {store_id: quantity} says. Returns its product_id."""
    conn = connect()
    try:
        with conn, conn.cursor() as cur:
            cur.execute(
                """
                INSERT INTO product (sku, name, brand_id, category_id, base_price)
                SELECT %s, 'Catalogue test speaker', b.brand_id, c.category_id, %s
                FROM brand b, category c
                WHERE b.name = 'JBL' AND c.name = 'Speakers'
                RETURNING product_id
                """,
                (unique_sku(), base_price),
            )
            product_id = cur.fetchone()["product_id"]
            if sale_price is not None:
                cur.execute(
                    """
                    INSERT INTO promotion (product_id, sale_price, starts_at, ends_at)
                    VALUES (%s, %s, now() - interval '1 day', now() + interval '1 day')
                    """,
                    (product_id, sale_price),
                )
            for store_id, quantity in (stock or {}).items():
                cur.execute("INSERT INTO inventory (store_id, product_id, quantity) VALUES (%s, %s, %s)",
                            (store_id, product_id, quantity))
    finally:
        conn.close()
    return product_id


def listed(**filters) -> dict:
    """GET /products with these query parameters -> {product_id: product}."""
    response = client.get(f"{API}/products", params=filters)
    assert response.status_code == 200, response.text
    return {product["product_id"]: product for product in response.json()}


# ---------------------------------------------------------------- price (R1, R2)

def test_the_price_filter_uses_the_current_price_not_the_base_price():
    on_sale = a_product(base_price="100.00", sale_price="80.00")
    # The sale price is inside the range, the base price outside it: listed, at the price it sells for.
    found = listed(min_price="75.00", max_price="85.00")
    assert on_sale in found
    assert (found[on_sale]["current_price"], found[on_sale]["on_sale"]) == ("80.00", True)
    # The base price is inside the range, the sale price outside it: not listed.
    assert on_sale not in listed(min_price="95.00", max_price="105.00")


def test_price_bounds_are_inclusive():
    product = a_product(base_price="80.00")
    assert product in listed(min_price="80.00", max_price="80.00")
    assert product not in listed(min_price="80.01")
    assert product not in listed(max_price="79.99")


def test_a_malformed_price_range_is_refused():
    inverted = client.get(f"{API}/products", params={"min_price": "100.00", "max_price": "50.00"})
    assert error_of(inverted) == (422, "invalid_price_range")
    for malformed in ({"min_price": "-1"}, {"max_price": "cheap"}, {"min_price": "1.005"}):
        assert error_of(client.get(f"{API}/products", params=malformed)) == (422, "validation_error")


# ---------------------------------------------------------------- categories (R1)

def test_a_top_level_category_includes_its_subcategories():
    categories = {c["name"]: c["category_id"] for c in client.get(f"{API}/categories").json()}
    audio = listed(category_id=categories["Audio"])
    headphones = listed(category_id=categories["Headphones"])
    # The seed puts no product in Audio itself: its list is its subcategories' products.
    audio_categories = {product["category"] for product in audio.values()}
    assert {"Headphones", "Speakers"} <= audio_categories <= {"Audio", "Headphones", "Speakers"}
    assert {product["category"] for product in headphones.values()} == {"Headphones"}
    assert set(headphones) < set(audio)


# ---------------------------------------------------------------- one store's stock (R3)

def test_store_quantity_is_the_stores_own_and_null_without_a_store():
    product = a_product(stock={1: 7, 2: 3})
    assert listed(store_id=1)[product]["store_quantity"] == 7
    assert listed(store_id=2)[product]["store_quantity"] == 3
    # With other filters as well: the store's parameter has to come first in the query.
    assert listed(store_id=2, max_price="100.00", q="Catalogue test")[product]["store_quantity"] == 3
    everywhere = listed()[product]
    assert (everywhere["store_quantity"], everywhere["total_stock"]) == (None, 10)
    assert client.get(f"{API}/products/{product}").json()["store_quantity"] is None


def test_a_store_without_an_inventory_row_shows_zero():
    product = a_product(stock={2: 5})                      # no inventory row at store 1
    assert listed(store_id=1)[product]["store_quantity"] == 0   # still listed: stock is information, not a filter


def test_an_unknown_store_is_not_found():
    assert error_of(client.get(f"{API}/products", params={"store_id": 999999})) == (404, "not_found")
```
